The slope (m) of a line passing through two points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\) is calculated using the formula:

\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]

For the given points (6, 4) and (-2, 8), we have:

- \( y_2 = 8 \)
- \( y_1 = 4 \)
- \( x_2 = -2 \)
- \( x_1 = 6 \)

Plugging these into the formula:

\[
m = \frac{8 - 4}{-2 - 6} = \frac{4}{-8} = -0.5
\]

So, the slope of the line is \(-0.5\).

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Coordinates:** The two points are A(5, 0) and B(9, 0).
2. **Apply the Slope Formula:** 
   \[
   m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} = \frac{0 - 0}{9 - 5}
   \]
3. **Calculate Numerator and Denominator:**

   - Denominator (\(x_2 - x_1\)) = 9 - 5 = 4
4. **Compute the Slope:**
   \[

   \]

Okay, so I have this problem where there are two lines, and the angle between them is π/4 radians. One line has a slope of 1/4, and I need to find the slope of the other line. Hmm, let's think about how to approach this.

First, I remember that the formula for the angle θ between two lines with slopes m₁ and m₂ is given by tanθ = |(m₂ - m₁)/(1 + m₁*m₂)|. In this case, θ is π/4, which means tanθ is 1 because tan(π/4) equals 1.

So plugging in the values I have: 1 = |(m₂ - (1/4))/(1 + (1/4)*m₂)|. Now, since it's an absolute value equation, there are two possibilities for this equation. Either the expression inside is positive or negative.

Let me write both cases:

Case 1:
(m₂ - 1/4) / (1 + (1/4)m₂) = 1

Cross-multiplying gives m₂ - 1/4 = 1 + (1/4)m₂. Let's solve for m₂.

Bring all terms to one side: m₂ - (1/4)m₂ = 1 + 1/4
That simplifies to (3/4)m₂ = 5/4

So, m₂ = (5/4) / (3/4) = 5/3.

Case 2:
(m₂ - 1/4) / (1 + (1/4)m₂) = -1

Cross-multiplying gives m₂ - 1/4 = -1 - (1/4)m₂.

Bring all terms to one side: m₂ + (1/4)m₂ = -1 + 1/4
That simplifies to (5/4)m₂ = -3/4

So, m₂ = (-3/4) / (5/4) = -3/5.

Therefore, the two possible slopes are 5/3 and -3/5. Both of these satisfy the condition that the angle between them is π/4.

To determine if the points \((-2, -1)\), \((4, 0)\), and \((3, 3)\) lie on the same straight line without using distance formulas, follow these steps:

### Step 1: Calculate the Slope Between Two Points
First, find the slope between two of the given points. Let's choose \((-2, -1)\) and \((4, 0)\):

\[
\text{Slope (m)} = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} = \frac{0 - (-1)}{4 - (-2)} = \frac{1}{6}
\]

### Step 2: Find the Equation of the Line
Using the point-slope form with one of the points (e.g., \((-2, -1)\)):

\[
y - y_1 = m(x - x_1) \\
y - (-1) = \frac{1}{6}(x - (-2)) \\
y + 1 = \frac{1}{6}(x + 2) \\
y = \frac{1}{6}x + \frac{2}{6} - 1 \\
y = \frac{1}{6}x - \frac{4}{6}
\]

Simplify the equation:

\[
y = \frac{1}{6}x - \frac{2}{3}
\]

### Step 3: Check if All Points Lie on the Same Line
Verify if the third point \((3, 3)\) satisfies this equation:

Substitute \(x = 3\) into the equation:

\[
y = \frac{1}{6}(3) - \frac{2}{3} \\
y = \frac{3}{6} - \frac{4}{6} \\
y = -\frac{1}{6}
\]

The calculated \(y\)-value is \(-\frac{1}{6}\), which does not match the given \(y = 3\) for the point \((3, 3)\).

### Conclusion
Since all three points do **not** lie on the same straight line:

The equation of the straight line passing through the points (3, 1) and (5, 7) can be found using the two-point formula.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Calculate the Slope (m):**
   - Points: A(3, 1) and B(5, 7)
   - Slope formula: m = (y2 - y1)/(x2 - x1)
   - Substituting values:
     m = (7 - 1)/(5 - 3) = 6/2 = 3

   - Using point-slope form: y - y1 = m(x - x1)
   - Using point A(3, 1):
     1 = 3*3 + c
     1 = 9 + c ⇒ c = 1 - 9 = -8

3. **Equation of the Line in Slope-Intercept Form:**
   y = mx + c
   y = 3x - 8

4. **Standard Form (Optional):**
   Bring all terms to one side:
   3x - y - 8 = 0

**Final Answer:**  
The equation of the line is {eq}\boxed{y = 3x - 8} {/eq}.

The problem asks for the equation of a line where the tangent of theta equals 1/4 and the y-intercept is at -5/4. 

Step-by-Step Explanation:

1. **Identify tan(theta)**: Given that tan(theta) = 1/4, this corresponds to the slope (m) of the line.

2. **Determine the equation form**: The general equation for a straight line in slope-intercept form is y = mx + b, where m is the slope and b is the y-intercept.

3. **Substitute known values**:
   - Slope (m) = 1/4
   - Y-intercept (b) = -5/4

4. **Formulate the equation**: Plugging these into the slope-intercept formula gives:

y = (1/4)x - 5/4.

**Answer:**

The equation of the line is y = \(\frac{1}{4}x - \frac{5}{4}\).

First, I need to determine if three points are collinear. Collinear means all three points lie on the same straight line.

To check this, I'll use the slope formula between pairs of points. If the slopes between each pair of points are equal, then the points are collinear.

Let's calculate the slope between point A (3, 0) and point B (-2, -2):
Slope AB = (y₂ - y₁) / (x₂ - x₁) = (-2 - 0) / (-2 - 3) = (-2) / (-5) = 0.4

Next, calculate the slope between point B (-2, -2) and point C (8, 2):
Slope BC = (2 - (-2)) / (8 - (-2)) = 4 / 10 = 0.4

Now, check if these slopes are equal:
Slope AB = Slope BC = 0.4
Since both slopes are the same, all three points lie on the same straight line.

Therefore, the given points (3, 0), (-2, -2), and (8, 2) are collinear.
</think>

To determine whether the points \((3, 0)\), \((-2, -2)\), and \((8, 2)\) are collinear using the concept of straight lines, we can follow these steps:

1. **Calculate the Slope Between Two Pairs of Points**:
   - **Slope between \(A(3, 0)\) and \(B(-2, -2)\)**:
     \[
     m_{AB} = \frac{y_B - y_A}{x_B - x_A} = \frac{-2 - 0}{-2 - 3} = \frac{-2}{-5} = \frac{2}{5}
     \]
   - **Slope between \(B(-2, -2)\) and \(C(8, 2)\)**:
     \[
     m_{BC} = \frac{y_C - y_B}{x_C - x_B} = \frac{2 - (-2)}{8 - (-2)} = \frac{4}{10} = \frac{2}{5}
     \]

2. **Compare the Slopes**:
   Since \(m_{AB} = m_{BC} = \frac{2}{5}\), all three points lie on the same straight line.

3. **Conclusion**:
   The points are collinear because the slopes between each pair of points are equal.

First, I need to determine the slope of the line passing through the point (0,2) and making an angle of 2 2/3 degrees with the positive x-axis. To find the slope, I'll convert this angle into radians since trigonometric functions typically use radians.

**Solution:**

We are given:
- A point through which the line passes: \( (0, 2) \)
- The angle that the line makes with the positive x-axis: \( 2\frac{2}{3}^\circ \)

### Step 1: Convert the Angle to Decimal
First, convert \( 2\frac{2}{3}^\circ \) to a decimal:
\[
2\frac{2}{3} = 2 + \frac{2}{3} = 2.6667^\circ
\]

### Step 2: Calculate the Slope (m)
The slope \( m \) of a line making an angle \( \theta \) with the positive x-axis is given by:
\[
m = \tan(\theta)
\]
So,
\[
m = \tan(2.6667^\circ) \approx 0.0463
\]

### Step 3: Find the Equation of the Line
Using the point-slope form of a line equation:
\[
y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)
\]
Substitute \( (x_1, y_1) = (0, 2) \):
\[
y - 2 = 0.0463(x - 0)
\]
Simplify to get the equation of the line:
\[
y = 0.0463x + 2
\]

### Step 4: Equation of a Parallel Line
A line parallel to the given line will have the same slope \( m \). Therefore, its general form is:
\[
y = 0.0463x + b
\]
To find \( b \) for the specific case (we need an additional condition), but since no specific point is provided for the parallel line, we'll assume it's also passing through a different point or simply use another y-intercept.

### Step 5: Find Where the Parallel Line Cuts the Y-Axis
For any line in the form \( y = mx + b \), it cuts the y-axis at \( (0, b) \). Since we're looking for the parallel line passing through a different point or with a different intercept, but without additional information, we'll assume finding another y-intercept based on some condition. However, since no specific new condition is provided, the primary focus here is on understanding that the slope remains \( 0.0463 \) and to identify how it intersects the y-axis.

For clarity, if we consider a general parallel line with the same slope but different intercept:
\[
y = 0.0463x + b'
\]
Where \( b' \) is another constant value.

**Final Answer:**

The equation of the original line is:
\[
\boxed{y = 0.0463x + 2}
\]

A parallel line will have the same slope but a different y-intercept, such as \( \boxed{y = 0.0463x + b'} \), where \( b' \) is another constant value determined by an additional condition.

Alright, after going through all those steps and double-checking each part, it's clear that when trying to find a line passing through the point (4,4) with intercepts adding up to 13, we end up with a quadratic equation that has no real solutions. This means there are no such lines that satisfy both conditions.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Equation Setup:**
   - The general form of a line's equation using intercepts is \( \frac{x}{a} + \frac{y}{b} = 1 \), where \( a \) is the x-intercept and \( b \) is the y-intercept.
   - Given that the sum of the intercepts is 13, we have \( a + b = 13 \).

2. **Line Passing Through (4,4):**
   - Substituting the point (4,4) into the line equation: \( \frac{4}{a} + \frac{4}{b} = 1 \).
   
3. **Expressing Variables:**
   - From \( a + b = 13 \), express one variable in terms of the other: \( b = 13 - a \).

4. **Substituting into Line Equation:**
   - Plug \( b = 13 - a \) into \( \frac{4}{a} + \frac{4}{b} = 1 \):
     \[
     \frac{4}{a} + \frac{4}{13 - a} = 1
     \]
   
5. **Combining Fractions:**
   - Combine the fractions by finding a common denominator:
     \[
     \frac{4(13 - a) + 4a}{a(13 - a)} = 1 \Rightarrow \frac{52}{a(13 - a)} = 1
     \]
   
6. **Solving for \( a \):**
   - This leads to the equation:
     \[
     a(13 - a) = 52 \Rightarrow 13a - a^2 = 52 \Rightarrow a^2 - 13a + 52 = 0
     \]
   
7. **Quadratic Equation Discriminant:**
   - Calculate the discriminant \( D = (-13)^2 - 4(1)(52) = 169 - 208 = -39 \).
   - Since \( D < 0 \), there are no real solutions for \( a \).

**Conclusion:**  
There are no such lines that pass through the point (4,4) and have intercepts summing to 13 because it leads to a quadratic equation with no real roots.

$\boxed{\text{No solution exists}}$

To find the distance from a point to a line given by its equation, I can use the formula that involves substituting the coordinates of the point into the line's equation.

First, identify the coefficients A, B, and C from the equation 5x - 6y -28 = 0. Here, A is 5, B is -6, and C is -28.

Next, plug in the values x₀ = 5 and y₀ = -7 into the distance formula: |A*x₀ + B*y₀ + C| divided by the square root of (A² + B²).

Calculate the numerator: 5*5 + (-6)*(-7) + (-28) which equals 25 + 42 - 28 = 39.

Then, calculate the denominator: sqrt(5² + (-6)²) = sqrt(25 + 36) = sqrt(61).

Finally, divide the numerator by the denominator to find the distance: 39 divided by sqrt(61), which simplifies to (39*sqrt(61))/61.

To calculate the distance from the point \((5, -7)\) to the line \(5x - 6y - 28 = 0\), we'll use the formula for the distance \(d\) from a point \((x_0, y_0)\) to a line \(Ax + By + C = 0\):

\[
d = \frac{|A x_0 + B y_0 + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
\]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Identify the coefficients from the line equation:**
   - \(A = 5\)
   - \(B = -6\)
   - \(C = -28\)

2. **Plug in the coordinates of the point into the formula:**
   \[
   d = \frac{|5(5) + (-6)(-7) - 28|}{\sqrt{5^2 + (-6)^2}}
   \]

3. **Calculate the numerator:**
   \[
   |5(5) + (-6)(-7) - 28| = |25 + 42 - 28| = |39|
   \]

4. **Calculate the denominator:**
   \[
   \sqrt{5^2 + (-6)^2} = \sqrt{25 + 36} = \sqrt{61}
   \]

5. **Compute the final distance:**
   \[
   d = \frac{39}{\sqrt{61}} = \frac{39 \sqrt{61}}{61}
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{\dfrac{39\,\sqrt{61}}{61}}

The distance between the two parallel lines is calculated using the formula for the distance between two parallel lines \( ax + by + c_1 = 0 \) and \( ax + by + c_2 = 0 \), which is:

\[
\text{Distance} = \frac{|c_1 - c_2|}{\sqrt{a^2 + b^2}}
\]

For the given lines:
- First line: \( 3x - 4y + 12 = 0 \) (where \( a = 3 \), \( b = -4 \), \( c_1 = 12 \))
- Second line: \( 3x - 4y + 7 = 0 \) (where \( a = 3 \), \( b = -4 \), \( c_2 = 7 \))

Plugging in the values:

\[
\text{Distance} = \frac{|12 - 7|}{\sqrt{3^2 + (-4)^2}} = \frac{5}{\sqrt{9 + 16}} = \frac{5}{5} = 1
\]

The area of triangle ABC with vertices A(4,5), B(6,1), and C(3,4) can be calculated using the shoelace formula:

Area = |(x₁(y₂ - y₃) + x₂(y₃ - y₁) + x₃(y₁ - y₂)) / 2|

Plugging in the coordinates:
= |(4*(1-4) + 6*(4-5) + 3*(5-1)) / 2|
= |(4*(-3) + 6*(-1) + 3*4)/2|
= |(-12 -6 +12)/2|
= |-6/2|
= 3

The area of triangle ABC is 3 square units.

The problem involves finding the slope \( m \) and y-intercept \( c \) of a line such that the perpendicular from the origin to this line intersects it at the point (2, 4).

1. **Determine the Perpendicular Slope**:
   - The given line has a slope \( m \).
   - The perpendicular slope is \( -\frac{1}{m} \) since perpendicular lines have slopes that multiply to -1.

2. **Equation of Perpendicular Line**:
   - This perpendicular line passes through the origin (0, 0), so its equation is \( y = -\frac{1}{m}x \).

3. **Substitute Intersection Point**:
   - The point (2, 4) lies on this perpendicular line.
   - Substituting into the equation: 
     \[
     4 = -\frac{1}{m}(2)
     \]
   - Solving for \( m \):
     \[
     4 = -\frac{2}{m} \Rightarrow m = -\frac{2}{4} = -\frac{1}{2}
     \]

4. **Find the Y-Intercept (\( c \))**:
   - The original line equation is \( y = mx + c \).
   - Substituting (2, 4) into this equation with \( m = -\frac{1}{2} \):
     \[
     4 = -\frac{1}{2}(2) + c
     \]
   - Simplifying:
     \[
     4 = -1 + c \Rightarrow c = 5
     \]

**Final Answer:**
The slope \( m \) is -0.5 and the y-intercept \( c \) is 5.

To find the value of \( H \), we start by noting that two lines are perpendicular if the product of their slopes equals \(-1\).

The given line is \(7x - 9y - 19 = 0\). Converting this to slope-intercept form:
\[
-9y = -7x + 19 \\
y = \frac{7}{9}x - \frac{19}{9}
\]
So, the slope (\(m_1\)) is \( \frac{7}{9} \).

For the line passing through points \((H,5)\) and \((6,3)\), the slope (\(m_2\)) is:
\[
m_2 = \frac{3 - 5}{6 - H} = \frac{-2}{6 - H}
\]

Setting \( m_1 \times m_2 = -1 \):
\[
\left(\frac{7}{9}\right) \times \left(\frac{-2}{6 - H}\right) = -1
\]
Simplifying:
\[
\frac{-14}{54 - 9H} = -1 \\
-14 = -54 + 9H \\
40 = 9H \\
H = \frac{40}{9}
\]

The value of \( H \) is \( \frac{40}{9} \).

The angle θ between the two lines can be found using their slopes. The formula used is tanθ = |(m2 - m1)/(1 + m1*m2)|. 

For the given lines:
- First line: sqrt(3)x + y = 1 ⇒ slope (m1) = -sqrt(3)
- Second line: x + sqrt(3)y = 1 ⇒ slope (m2) = -1/sqrt(3)

Plugging these into the formula:

tanθ = |(-1/sqrt(3) - (-sqrt(3)))/(1 + (-sqrt(3))*(-1/sqrt(3)))|
= |(sqrt(3) - 1/sqrt(3))/(1 + 1)|
= |(2/sqrt(3))/2|
= |1/sqrt(3)|

This gives θ = 30 degrees.

Actually, for two lines to be parallel, their normal vectors (the coefficients of x and y) must be scalar multiples of each other.

The second line is 38x + 24y +70 = 0.

To simplify the given equation into intercept form and find its intercepts:

**Given Equation:**  
Assuming it's meant to be linear:  
\(12x + 9y = 18\)

**Step-by-Step Solution:**  

1. **Divide both sides by 18** to get the equation in intercept form:
   \[
   \frac{12x}{18} + \frac{9y}{18} = \frac{18}{18}
   \]
   
2. **Simplify each term:**
   \[
   \frac{2x}{3} + \frac{y}{2} = 1
   \]

3. **Identify the intercepts** from the simplified equation:
   - **X-intercept (a):** Set \( y = 0 \):
     \[
     \frac{2x}{3} = 1 \implies x = \frac{3}{2}
     \]
     So, the x-intercept is at \( \left( \frac{3}{2}, 0 \right) \).
   
   - **Y-intercept (b):** Set \( x = 0 \):
     \[
     \frac{y}{2} = 1 \implies y = 2
     \]
     So, the y-intercept is at \( (0, 2) \).

**Final Answer:**  
The equation in intercept form is \( \frac{x}{\frac{3}{2}} + \frac{y}{2} = 1 \), with x-intercept at \( \left( \frac{3}{2}, 0 \right) \) and y-intercept at \( (0, 2) \).

The distance from the point (1, 2) to the line 4x + 7y + 5 = 0 is calculated using the formula for the distance from a point to a line in coordinate geometry:

**Formula:**  
Distance \( d \) from point \( (x_1, y_1) \) to line \( Ax + By + C = 0 \):  
\[ d = \frac{|Ax_1 + By_1 + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}} \]

**Given:**  
Point: \( (1, 2) \)  
Line equation: \( 4x + 7y + 5 = 0 \)

**Calculation:**  
Substitute the values into the formula:  
\[ d = \frac{|4(1) + 7(2) + 5|}{\sqrt{4^2 + 7^2}} = \frac{|4 + 14 + 5|}{\sqrt{16 + 49}} = \frac{23}{\sqrt{65}} \]

**Final Answer:**  
The distance is \( \frac{23}{\sqrt{65}} \).

To find the distance from the point \( P(4, 1) \) to the line \( 4x - y = 0 \), we can use the formula for the distance from a point to a straight line.

### Given:
- Point: \( (x_1, y_1) = (4, 1) \)
- Line equation: \( 4x - y = 0 \)

### Formula for Distance (\( d \)):
\[
d = \frac{|A x_1 + B y_1 + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
\]
Where:
- \( A = 4 \)
- \( B = -1 \)
- \( C = 0 \)

### Substituting the Values:
\[
d = \frac{|4(4) + (-1)(1) + 0|}{\sqrt{4^2 + (-1)^2}}
\]

\[
d = \frac{|16 - 1|}{\sqrt{16 + 1}} = \frac{15}{\sqrt{17}}
\]

### Final Answer:
The distance from point \( P(4, 1) \) to the line \( 4x - y = 0 \) is:

To determine the equation of a line parallel to both given lines, we start by noting that two lines are parallel if they have the same slope. 

The given lines are:
1. \(9x + 6y = 7\)
2. \(3x + 2y = -6\)

First, we convert these equations into slope-intercept form to find their slopes.

For the first line:
\[9x + 6y = 7\]
Subtract \(9x\) from both sides:
\[6y = -9x + 7\]
Divide by 6:
\[y = -\frac{3}{2}x + \frac{7}{6}\]

For the second line:
\[3x + 2y = -6\]
Subtract \(3x\) from both sides:
\[2y = -3x - 6\]
Divide by 2:
\[y = -\frac{3}{2}x - 3\]

Both lines have the same slope, \(-\frac{3}{2}\), indicating they are parallel.

To find a line parallel to both given lines, we use the form \(3x + 2y = k\) (since this is derived from dividing by the GCD of the coefficients for the second line). This form ensures the same slope but allows any constant term \(k\), making it different from the original lines.

Thus, the equation of a line parallel to both given lines is:
\[
\boxed{3x + 2y = k}
\]
where \(k\) is any real number except \(-6\) (to avoid coinciding with the second given line).

In this case, θ is π/4, so tanθ would be tan(π/4), which I recall is 1. One of the slopes, let's say m₁, is 1/4. So plugging in these values into the formula:

1 = |(m₂ - 1/4)/(1 + (1/4)*m₂)|

Now, since absolute value is involved, there are two possibilities: either the expression inside is positive or negative. So I'll have to solve both cases.

First case:
(m₂ - 1/4)/(1 + (1/4)m₂) = 1
Multiply both sides by denominator:
m₂ - 1/4 = 1*(1 + (1/4)m₂)
Expand the right side:
m₂ - 1/4 = 1 + (1/4)m₂
Bring all terms to left side:
m₂ - (1/4)m₂ -1/4 -1 =0
Factor m₂:
(3/4)m₂ -5/4=0
Add 5/4 to both sides:
(3/4)m₂ =5/4
Multiply both sides by 4/3:
m₂=(5/4)*(4/3)=5/3

Second case:
(m₂ -1/4)/(1 + (1/4)m₂) = -1
Again, multiply both sides by denominator:
m₂ -1/4 = -1*(1 + (1/4)m₂)
Expand right side:
m₂ -1/4 = -1 - (1/4)m₂
Bring all terms to left side:
m₂ + (1/4)m₂ -1/4 +1=0
Factor m₂:
(5/4)m₂ + 3/4=0

m₂=(3/4)*(4/5)= 3/5

To find the value of \( x \) such that the line passing through points \( (10, 14) \) and \( (x, 26) \) is perpendicular to the line passing through points \( (0, 8) \) and \( (6, 10) \), follow these steps:

**Step 1: Calculate the slope of the first line.**
The slope (\( m_1 \)) between two points \( (x_1, y_1) \) and \( (x_2, y_2) \) is given by:
\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]
For the points \( (0, 8) \) and \( (6, 10) \):
\[
m_1 = \frac{10 - 8}{6 - 0} = \frac{2}{6} = \frac{1}{3}
\]

**Step 2: Determine the slope of the perpendicular line.**
The slopes of two perpendicular lines (\( m_1 \) and \( m_2 \)) satisfy the relationship:
\[
m_1 \times m_2 = -1
\]
Given \( m_1 = \frac{1}{3} \), solve for \( m_2 \):
\[
\frac{1}{3} \times m_2 = -1 \\
m_2 = -3
\]

**Step 3: Calculate the slope of the second line.**
For the points \( (10, 14) \) and \( (x, 26) \), set up the equation for \( m_2 \):
\[
m_2 = \frac{26 - 14}{x - 10} = \frac{12}{x - 10}
\]
We know \( m_2 = -3 \), so:
\[
\frac{12}{x - 10} = -3
\]

**Step 4: Solve for \( x \).**
Multiply both sides by \( (x - 10) \):
\[
12 = -3(x - 10)
\]
Distribute the \( -3 \):
\[
12 = -3x + 30
\]
Subtract \( 30 \) from both sides:
\[
-18 = -3x
\]
Divide by \( -3 \):
\[
x = 6
\]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{6}
\]

Therefore, slope would be Δy / Δx = (0.5)/( -√3/2 )= (1/2)/(-√3/2) = -1/√3.

So the slope would be negative 1 over square root of 3. Rationalizing, that's equal to -√3/3.

The problem involves finding the slopes (m) of two lines where one slope is twice the other, and the tangent of the angle between them is 1/3.

Using the formula for the angle θ between two lines with slopes m₁ and m₂:

tan(θ) = |(m₂ - m₁)/(1 + m₁*m₂)|

Let’s denote the slopes as m and 2m. Substituting into the formula:

tan(θ) = (2m - m)/(1 + m*2m)  
= m/(1 + 2m²)

Given that tan(θ) = 1/3, we set up the equation:

m/(1 + 2m²) = 1/3

Cross-multiplying gives:

3m = 1 + 2m²  
2m² - 3m + 1 = 0

Factoring the quadratic equation:

(2m - 1)(m - 1) = 0

Thus, m can be either 1 or 1/2.

**Final Answer:**
The slopes of the lines are {1 and 2} when one slope is twice the other.

The line we are looking for is parallel to 4x - y = 10 and passes through the point (0, 5). 

Firstly, rearranging 4x - y = 10 into slope-intercept form gives y = 4x - 10. Therefore, the slope of this line is 4.

Since parallel lines have equal slopes, our desired line will also have a slope of 4.

Using the point-slope form with the point (0,5), we get:
y - 5 = 4(x - 0)  
Simplifying gives: y = 4x + 5

Thus, the equation of the required line is y = 4x + 5.

**Solution:**

We are given two points:
- Point A: (3, 1)
- Point B: (5, 7)

To find the equation of the line passing through these two points, follow these steps:

### Step 1: Calculate the Slope (m)
The formula for slope is:
\[ m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} \]

Substituting the given points:
\[
m = \frac{7 - 1}{5 - 3} = \frac{6}{2} = 3
\]

### Step 2: Use Point-Slope Form to Find the Equation
The point-slope form of a line is:
\[ y - y_1 = m(x - x_1) \]

Using **Point A** (3, 1):
\[
y - 1 = 3(x - 3)
\]

### Step 3: Simplify to Slope-Intercept Form
Simplify the equation:
\[
y - 1 = 3x - 9 \\
y = 3x - 8
\]

### Final Answer:
The equation of the line is:
\[ \boxed{y = 3x - 8} \]

**Final Answer**
The equation factors as \boxed{\left(x - \frac{S + \sqrt{S^2 - 4a}}{2}\right)\left(x - \frac{S - \sqrt{S^2 - 4a}}{2}\right)}, where \( S = \sum_{k=1}^{n} \frac{1}{k^2 + a} \).
</think>

To solve the given equation, we start with:

\[ x \cdot \sum_{k=1}^{n} \frac{1}{k^2 + a} = x^2 + a \]

We denote the sum \( S = \sum_{k=1}^{n} \frac{1}{k^2 + a} \). Substituting this into the equation, we get:

\[ xS = x^2 + a \]

Rearranging all terms to one side, we obtain a quadratic equation:

\[ x^2 - xS + a = 0 \]

This quadratic equation can be factored using its roots. The roots of the quadratic equation \( x^2 - Sx + a = 0 \) are given by the quadratic formula:

\[ x = \frac{S \pm \sqrt{S^2 - 4a}}{2} \]

Thus, the quadratic equation can be factored as:

\[ (x - r_1)(x - r_2) = 0 \]

where \( r_1 = \frac{S + \sqrt{S^2 - 4a}}{2} \) and \( r_2 = \frac{S - \sqrt{S^2 - 4a}}{2} \).

Therefore, the factorization of the equation is:

\[ \boxed{\left(x - \frac{S + \sqrt{S^2 - 4a}}{2}\right)\left(x - \frac{S - \sqrt{S^2 - 4a}}{2}\right)} \]

where \( S = \sum_{k=1}^{n} \frac{1}{k^2 + a} \).

Given the problem where the quantity sold (Q) increased from 1080 to 1320 when the price (P) increased by Rs 2, we need to determine the linear relationship between Q and P.

First, we calculate the change in quantity sold and the change in price:
- Change in quantity sold (\(\Delta Q\)) = 1320 - 1080 = 240
- Change in price (\(\Delta P\)) = 2

The slope (m) of the linear relationship is calculated as:
\[ m = \frac{\Delta Q}{\Delta P} = \frac{240}{2} = 120 \]

Using the point-slope form of the linear equation with the point (114, 1080):
\[ Q - 1080 = 120(P - 114) \]

Expanding and simplifying this equation:
\[ Q = 120P - 120 \times 114 + 1080 \]
\[ Q = 120P - 13680 + 1080 \]
\[ Q = 120P - 12600 \]

Thus, the linear equation is:
\[ Q = 120P - 12600 \]

From this equation, we identify the coefficients \(a\) and \(b\):
- The intercept \(a\) is -12600
- The slope \(b\) is 120

### Final Answer
The values of \(a\) and \(b\) are \(\boxed{-12600}\) and \(\boxed{120}\), respectively.

To find the equation of the line passing through (0, 2) and making an angle of \(2\frac{2}{3}\)° with the positive x-axis:

1. **Calculate the slope**:  
   The slope \(m\) is given by \(\tan(2\frac{2}{3}^\circ)\).  
   \(m = \tan\left(\frac{8}{3}^\circ\right) \approx 0.04645\).

2. **Equation of the line**:  
   Using the point-slope form, since it passes through (0, 2):  
   \(y = mx + b\) where \(b = 2\).  
   Thus, \(y = 0.04645x + 2\).

3. **Equation of the parallel line**:  
   Since it is parallel, it has the same slope \(m \approx 0.04645\), but passes through a point two units below (0, 2) on the y-axis:  
   The new point is (0, 0). Using the same formula:  
   \(y = 0.04645x + 0\) or simply \(y = 0.04645x\).

**Final Answer:**  
The equations of the lines are \(y = 0.04645x + 2\) and \(y = 0.04645x\).

To find the equation of a line that passes through points dividing segment AB internally in some ratio \( n:1 \) and is perpendicular to AB, we start by noting:

- The slope of AB is 3, so any perpendicular has a slope of \( -\frac{1}{3} \).
- A point P on AB dividing it in the ratio \( n:1 \) from B can be expressed as:
  \[
  P = \left( \frac{2n + 1}{n+1}, \frac{3n}{n+1} \right)
  \]
  
The equation of the line through point P with slope \( -\frac{1}{3} \) is:

\[
y - \frac{3n}{n+1} = -\frac{1}{3}\left(x - \frac{2n + 1}{n+1}\right)
\]

Simplifying this, we get the final equation in terms of \( n \):

\[
(n+1)(x + 3y) = 11n + 1
\]

So, putting it all together:

The equation is:
\boxed{(n+1)x + 3(n+1)y = 11n + 1}

To find the equation of a line with slope \(-4\) that passes through an \(x\)-intercept and is at a distance of \(5\) units from the origin, we start by using the general form of the line equation: 

\( y = -4x + c \).

Since it passes through the \(x\)-intercept \((h, 0)\), substituting into the equation gives:

\( 0 = -4h + c \)  
Thus, \( c = 4h \).

The distance from the origin to this line is given by the formula for the distance from a point \((0,0)\) to the line \( ax + by + c = 0 \):

\[ \text{Distance} = \frac{|c|}{\sqrt{a^2 + b^2}}. \]

For our line rearranged as \(4x + y - c = 0\), this becomes:

\[ \text{Distance} = \frac{|-c|}{\sqrt{16 + 1}} = \frac{|c|}{\sqrt{17}}. \]

Setting this equal to \(5\) units gives:

\[ \frac{|c|}{\sqrt{17}} = 5 \]  
Thus, \( |c| = 5\sqrt{17} \), so \( c = \pm5\sqrt{17} \).

Substituting back into \( y = -4x + c \):

\[ y = -4x + 5\sqrt{17} \quad \text{or} \quad y = -4x - 5\sqrt{17}. \]

**Answer:** The equations are \( y = -4x + 5\sqrt{17} \) and \( y = -4x - 5\sqrt{17} \).

First, I need to determine the perpendicular distance from the point (5, -7) to the line defined by the equation 5x - 6y -28 = 0.

I recall that the formula for calculating the distance from a point \((x_1, y_1)\) to the line \(Ax + By + C = 0\) is:

\[
d = \frac{|A x_1 + B y_1 + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
\]

Next, I'll identify the coefficients from the given equation:
- \(A = 5\)
- \(B = -6\)
- \(C = -28\)

Now, substitute these values along with the point (5, -7) into the distance formula:

\[
d = \frac{|5(5) + (-6)(-7) - 28|}{\sqrt{5^2 + (-6)^2}} = \frac{|25 + 42 - 28|}{\sqrt{25 + 36}} = \frac{39}{\sqrt{61}}
\]

\[
d = \frac{39 \sqrt{61}}{61}
\]

To determine the perpendicular distance from the point (5, -7) to the line defined by \( 5x - 6y -28 = 0 \), we'll use the formula for the distance from a point to a straight line.

### Step-by-Step Solution:

1. **Identify the coefficients:**
   The given line equation is in the standard form \( Ax + By + C = 0 \).
   
   Here,
   - \( A = 5 \)
   - \( B = -6 \)
   - \( C = -28 \)

2. **Apply the distance formula:**
   The formula to find the perpendicular distance (\( d \)) from a point \((x_1, y_1)\) to the line is:
   
   \[
   d = \frac{|A x_1 + B y_1 + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
   \]
   
3. **Substitute the known values into the formula:**
   - \( A = 5 \)
   - \( B = -6 \)
   - \( C = -28 \)
   - \( x_1 = 5 \)
   - \( y_1 = -7 \)
   
   Substitute these into the formula:

   \[
   d = \frac{|5(5) + (-6)(-7) - 28|}{\sqrt{5^2 + (-6)^2}}
   \]
   
4. **Calculate the numerator:**
   
   \[
   |5(5) + (-6)(-7) - 28| = |25 + 42 - 28| = |39| = 39
   \]
   
5. **Calculate the denominator:**
   
   \[
   \sqrt{5^2 + (-6)^2} = \sqrt{25 + 36} = \sqrt{61}
   \]
   
6. **Finalize the distance calculation:**
   
   \[
   d = \frac{39}{\sqrt{61}}
   \]
   
7. **Rationalize the denominator (if needed):**
   
   To rationalize:
   
   \[
   d = \frac{39 \times \sqrt{61}}{\sqrt{61} \times \sqrt{61}} = \frac{39\sqrt{61}}{61}
   \]
   
### Final Answer:

The perpendicular distance from the point (5, -7) to the line \( 5x - 6y -28 = 0 \) is:

\[
\boxed{\dfrac{39}{\sqrt{61}}}
\]

The distance between the two parallel lines can be calculated using the formula for the distance between two parallel straight lines. 

Formula: 
\[ d = \frac{|c_2 - c_1|}{\sqrt{a^2 + b^2}} \]

Given equations:
- First line: \( 3x - 4y + 12 = 0 \) (where \( a = 3 \), \( b = -4 \), \( c_1 = 12 \))
- Second line: \( 3x - 4y + 7 = 0 \) (where \( a = 3 \), \( b = -4 \), \( c_2 = 7 \))

Calculations:
\[ d = \frac{|7 - 12|}{\sqrt{3^2 + (-4)^2}} = \frac{5}{5} = 1 \]

The distance between the two parallel lines is 1.

The values of \( m \) and \( c \) for the line \( y = mx + c \), given that a perpendicular from the origin passes through the point (2, 4), are found by determining that the slope of the original line is -1/2 and the y-intercept is 5.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Understand the Problem:** A perpendicular line from the origin to \( y = mx + c \) passes through (2, 4). 

2. **Find Slope of Perpendicular Line:** The slope between (0,0) and (2,4) is \( m_{\text{perp}} = \frac{4 - 0}{2 - 0} = 2 \).

3. **Relate to Original Line's Slope:** Since the perpendicular line has a slope of 2, the original line must have a slope \( m = -1/2 \) because they are negative reciprocals.

4. **Determine Y-Intercept (\( c \)):** The original line passes through (0,5), so substituting into \( y = mx + c \):
   \[
   5 = (-1/2)(0) + c \implies c = 5
   \]

Thus, the values are \( m = -\frac{1}{2} \) and \( c = 5 \).

**Answer:**  
The value of \( m \) is \(-\frac{1}{2}\) and the value of \( c \) is 5.

Now, let me explain step by step how we arrived at the foot of the perpendicular from point (1,5) onto the line 3x -4y=16:

**Step 1: Determine the slope of the given line.**
The equation is 3x - y = 16, which can be rewritten as y = 3x -16. Thus, the slope (m) of this line is 3.

**Step 2: Find the slope of the perpendicular line.**
The slope of a line perpendicular to another is the negative reciprocal of the original slope. So, m_perpendicular = -1/3.

**Step 3: Write the equation of the perpendicular line passing through (1,5).**
Using point-slope form:
y - 5 = (-1/3)(x - 1)
Simplifying:
y = (-1/3)x + (1/3) + 5
y = (-1/3)x + (16/3)

**Step 4: Find the intersection point of the two lines.**
We have two equations:
1. y = 3x -16
2. y = (-1/3)x + 16/3

Set them equal to each other:
3x -16 = (-1/3)x +16/3

Multiply all terms by 3 to eliminate denominators:
9x -48 = -x +16

Combine like terms:
9x + x = 16 +48
10x=64
x=6.4 or as a fraction, 32/5.

To solve the problem of finding the equation of the line that makes equal angles with the x-axis, we start by considering the slopes \( m_1 \) and \( m_2 \) of two given lines. Using the formula for angle bisectors between two lines:

\[
\frac{a_1 x + b_1 y + c_1}{\sqrt{a_1^2 + b_1^2}} = \pm \frac{a_2 x + b_2 y + c_2}{\sqrt{a_2^2 + b_2^2}}
\]

However, since the problem is about a single line making equal angles with the x-axis, we consider the slopes \( m \) and \( -m \). The key steps involve setting up the equation for the angle bisectors and solving the quadratic equation derived from it.

After solving the quadratic equation, we find the roots which give us the slopes of the angle bisectors. These slopes are then used to write the equations of the lines.

The final answer is:

\[
\boxed{y = (\sqrt{5} - 3)x}
\]

and another line with slope equal to the other root of the quadratic equation, but due to lack of exact values, we conclude with this result.

***********************<think>
First, I'll convert both equations to slope-intercept form to find their slopes.

For the first line:
√3x + y = 1 → y = -√3x + 1
Slope (m₁) = -√3

For the second line:
x + √3y = 1 → y = (-1/√3)x + 1/√3
Slope (m₂) = -1/√3

Next, I'll use the formula for the angle θ between two lines:
tanθ = |(m₂ - m₁)/(1 + m₁*m₂)|

Substituting the slopes:
tanθ = |((-1/√3) - (-√3))/(1 + (-√3)*(-1/√3))| = |(2/√3)/(2)| = 1/√3

Thus, θ = arctan(1/√3) = 30 degrees.
</think>

To find the angle between the two lines given by the equations:

\[
\sqrt{3}x + y = 1 \quad \text{and} \quad x + \sqrt{3}y = 1
\]

**Step 1: Convert Each Line to Slope-Intercept Form**

For the first line:
\[
\sqrt{3}x + y = 1 \\
\Rightarrow y = -\sqrt{3}x + 1
\]
The slope (\(m_1\)) is \(-\sqrt{3}\).

For the second line:
\[
x + \sqrt{3}y = 1 \\
\Rightarrow \sqrt{3}y = -x + 1 \\
\Rightarrow y = \left(\frac{-1}{\sqrt{3}}\right)x + \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}
\]
The slope (\(m_2\)) is \(-\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\).

**Step 2: Use the Formula for the Angle Between Two Lines**

The formula to find the angle (\(\theta\)) between two lines with slopes \(m_1\) and \(m_2\) is:

\[
\tan\theta = \left| \frac{m_2 - m_1}{1 + m_1 \cdot m_2} \right|
\]

Substitute the values of \(m_1\) and \(m_2\):

\[
\tan\theta = \left| \frac{-\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}} - (-\sqrt{3})}{1 + (-\sqrt{3}) \cdot \left(-\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\right)} \right| \\
= \left| \frac{\frac{-1}{\sqrt{3}} + \sqrt{3}}{1 + 1} \right| \\
= \left| \frac{\frac{-1 + 3}{\sqrt{3}}}{2} \right| \\
= \left| \frac{\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}}{2} \right| \\
= \left| \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}} \right|
\]

**Step 3: Calculate the Angle**

\[
\tan\theta = \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}} \\
\Rightarrow \theta = \arctan\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\right) \\
\Rightarrow \theta = 30^\circ
\]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{\text{Angle between the lines is } 30^\circ}

First, I recognize that the problem requires finding the equation of a line parallel to the given line \(5x - 6y + 4 = 0\) and passing through the point \((-4, 5)\).

</think>

To find the equation of a line **parallel** to \(5x - 6y + 4 = 0\) and passing through the point \((-4, 5)\), follow these steps:

1. **Find the Slope of the Given Line:**

   The given line is:
   \[
   5x - 6y + 4 = 0
   \]
   
   Rearrange to slope-intercept form (\(y = mx + b\)):
   \[
   -6y = -5x - 4 \\
   y = \frac{5}{6}x + \frac{2}{3}
   \]
   
   **Slope (\(m\))**: \(\frac{5}{6}\)

2. **Use the Point-Slope Form:**

   The point-slope formula is:
   \[
   y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)
   \]
   
   Plug in \(m = \frac{5}{6}\) and the point \((-4, 5)\):
   \[
   y - 5 = \frac{5}{6}(x + 4)
   \]

3. **Simplify to Standard Form:**

   Multiply both sides by 6 to eliminate fractions:
   \[
   6(y - 5) = 5(x + 4)
   \]
   
   Expand and simplify:
   \[
   6y - 30 = 5x + 20 \\
   -5x + 6y - 50 = 0
   \]

   The equation of the parallel line is:
   \[
   5x - 6y + (-50) = 0
   \]
   
   To match standard form, it's often written as:
   \[
   5x - 6y - 50 = 0
   \]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{5x - 6y - 50 = 0}
\]

   \[

Simplifying: y = (-3/4)x + 9/4

Both lines have the same slope of -3/4, indicating they are parallel.

Since the slopes are equal, I can use the distance formula for parallel lines:
Distance (d) = |c2 - c1| / √(a² + b²)

Rewriting both equations to match standard form ax + by + c = 0:

First line: 3x + 4y - 9 = 0 → a=3, b=4, c=-9
Second line: 6x + 8y -18 = 0 → Here, I can factor out 2 to get it in terms of the first equation. Dividing both sides by 2 gives us 3x + 4y -9 =0.

To determine the distance between the two straight lines given by the equations:

1. \( 3x + 4y = 9 \)
2. \( 6x + 8y = 18 \)

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

### Step 1: Check if the Lines are Parallel
First, we'll check if the two lines are parallel by comparing their slopes.

For the first line:
\[
3x + 4y = 9 \\
4y = -3x + 9 \\
y = \left(-\frac{3}{4}\right)x + \frac{9}{4}
\]
The slope (\(m_1\)) is \( -\frac{3}{4} \).

For the second line:
\[
6x + 8y = 18 \\
8y = -6x + 18 \\
y = \left(-\frac{6}{8}\right)x + \frac{18}{8} \\
y = \left(-\frac{3}{4}\right)x + \frac{9}{4}
\]
The slope (\(m_2\)) is \( -\frac{3}{4} \).

Since both slopes are equal, the lines are **parallel**.

### Step 2: Use the Distance Formula for Parallel Lines
The formula to find the distance (\(d\)) between two parallel lines \( ax + by + c = 0 \) and \( ax + by + d = 0 \) is:
\[
d = \frac{|c - d|}{\sqrt{a^2 + b^2}}
\]

First, rewrite both equations in the standard form (\(ax + by + c = 0\)):

1. \( 3x + 4y - 9 = 0 \)
2. Dividing the second equation by 2 to match the coefficients of \(x\) and \(y\) with the first line:
   \[
   \frac{6x + 8y - 18}{2} = 0 \\
   3x + 4y - 9 = 0
   \]
   
Now, both equations are identical except for the constant term. This means they represent the **same** line.

### Step 3: Calculate the Distance
Since both lines are identical (\(c_1 = c_2\)), the distance between them is:
\[
d = \frac{| -9 - (-9) |}{\sqrt{3^2 + 4^2}} \\
d = \frac{0}{5} \\
d = 0
\]

### Final Answer

The two lines are identical, so the distance between them is zero.

**Answer:**
\[
\boxed{\text{Distance Between Lines} = 0}
\]

   - Second point: \((x_2, y_2) = (-2, 8)\)

   \[

Okay, so I have this problem where there are two lines, and the angle between them is π/4 radians, which is 45 degrees. One of the lines has a slope (or gradient) of 1/4, and I need to find the slope of the other line.

First, I remember that when dealing with the angle between two lines, there's a formula involving their slopes. The formula relates the tangent of the angle between them to the difference in their slopes divided by one plus the product of the slopes. So, if m1 and m2 are the slopes of the two lines, then tan(θ) = |(m2 - m1)/(1 + m1*m2)|.

In this case, θ is π/4, which means tan(π/4) is 1 because I remember that tan(45 degrees) is 1. So, substituting the known values into the formula: 1 = |(m2 - (1/4))/(1 + (1/4)*m2)|.

Now, since we're dealing with absolute value, there are two possibilities here: either (m2 - 1/4)/(1 + m2/4) equals 1 or it equals -1. So I need to consider both cases separately.

Let me start with the first case where the fraction is positive:

(m2 - 1/4) / (1 + m2/4) = 1

Multiplying both sides by the denominator gives:

m2 - 1/4 = 1*(1 + m2/4)

Expanding the right side: 1*1 is 1, and 1*(m2/4) is m2/4.

So, m2 - 1/4 = 1 + (m2)/4

Now, I can move all terms involving m2 to one side and constants to the other:

m2 - (m2)/4 = 1 + 1/4

Let's compute the left side: m2 is the same as 4m2/4, so subtracting m2/4 gives (4m2 - m2)/4 = 3m2/4.

On the right side, adding 1 and 1/4 gives 5/4.

So now we have:

3m2/4 = 5/4

Multiplying both sides by 4 to eliminate denominators:

3m2 = 5

Dividing both sides by 3:

m2 = 5/3

Okay, that's one possible slope. Now let me check the second case where the fraction is negative:

(m2 - 1/4) / (1 + m2/4) = -1

Again, multiply both sides by the denominator:

m2 - 1/4 = -1*(1 + m2/4)

Expanding the right side: -1*1 is -1 and -1*(m2)/4 is -m2/4.

So now we have:

m2 - 1/4 = -1 - (m2)/4

Again, move all terms with m2 to one side and constants to the other:

m2 + (m2)/4 = -1 + 1/4

Left side: m2 is 4m2/4, so adding m2/4 gives (4m2 + m2)/4 = 5m2/4.

Right side: -1 is -4/4, so -4/4 + 1/4 is (-4+1)/4 = -3/4.

So now:

5m2/4 = -3/4

Multiply both sides by 4:

5m2 = -3

Divide by 5:

m2 = -3/5

First case: m2 = 5/3

tan(theta) = |(5/3 - 1/4)/(1 + (1/4)*(5/3))|

Compute numerator: 5/3 - 1/4. Let's find a common denominator, which is 12:

(5*4)/(3*4) = 20/12

(1*3)/(4*3)=3/12

So, 20/12 - 3/12 = 17/12.

Denominator: 1 + (1/4)*(5/3) = 1 + 5/12. Convert 1 to 12/12:

12/12 + 5/12 = 17/12

So, tan(theta)= |(17/12)/(17/12)|=|1|=1, which is correct since theta is π/4.

Second case: m2 = -3/5

tan(theta) = |(-3/5 - 1/4)/(1 + (1/4)*(-3/5))|

Compute numerator:

-3/5 - 1/4. Common denominator is 20:

(-3*4)/20 = -12/20

(1*5)/20=5/20

So, total numerator: -12/20 -5/20 = -17/20.

Denominator:

1 + (1/4)*(-3/5) = 1 - 3/20. Convert 1 to 20/20:

20/20 - 3/20=17/20

So tan(theta)= |(-17/20)/(17/20)|=| -1 | =1, which also checks out.

Great, so both solutions are valid. Therefore, the possible slopes for the second line are indeed 5/3 and -3/5.
</think>

In this problem, tanθ is 1/3, so I can set up the equation as:

| (m₂ - m₁)/(1 + m₁m₂) | = 1/3

First, I need to determine the equation of a straight line passing through two given points, (3, 1) and (5, 7).

To find the slope \( m \), I'll use the formula:
\[ m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} = \frac{7 - 1}{5 - 3} = \frac{6}{2} = 3 \]

With the slope known, I can use the point-slope form of a line's equation:
\[ y - y_1 = m(x - x_1) \]
Substituting one of the points, say (3, 1):
\[ y - 1 = 3(x - 3) \]

Finally, simplifying this to get the standard form:
\[ y - 1 = 3x - 9 \]
\[ y = 3x - 8 \]

Thus, the equation of the line is \( y = 3x - 8 \).
</think>

To find the equation of a straight line passing through two points (3, 1) and (5, 7), we can follow these steps:

**Step 1: Calculate the Slope (m)**  
The slope \( m \) between two points \( (x_1, y_1) \) and \( (x_2, y_2) \) is given by:
\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]
Substituting the given points:
\[
m = \frac{7 - 1}{5 - 3} = \frac{6}{2} = 3
\]

**Step 2: Use the Point-Slope Form to Find the Equation of the Line**  
The point-slope form of a line's equation is:
\[
y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)
\]
Using one of the points, for example, (3, 1):
\[
y - 1 = 3(x - 3)
\]

**Step 3: Simplify to Slope-Intercept Form**  
Simplify the equation:
\[
y - 1 = 3x - 9 \\
y = 3x - 8
\]
This is the equation of the line in slope-intercept form.

**Final Answer:**  
The equation of the straight line passing through points (3, 1) and (5, 7) is:
\[
\boxed{y = 3x - 8}
\]

- The x-intercept \( a = -1 \)
- The y-intercept \( b = 4 \)

Plugging these values into the equation gives:
\( \frac{x}{-1} + \frac{y}{4} = 1 \)

Simplifying this, I get:
\( -x + \frac{y}{4} = 1 \)

To eliminate the fraction, multiply every term by 4:
\( -4x + y = 4 \)

Rearranging terms to present it in a standard form:
\( 4x - y + 4 = 0 \)
</think>

**Solution:**

To find the equation of the straight line that intersects the x-axis at \(-1\) and the y-axis at \(4\), we can use the **intercept form** of a straight line:

\[
\frac{x}{a} + \frac{y}{b} = 1
\]

where:
- \(a\) is the x-intercept,
- \(b\) is the y-intercept.

Given:
- X-intercept (\(a\)) = \(-1\),
- Y-intercept (\(b\)) = \(4\).

Substitute these values into the intercept form:

\[
\frac{x}{-1} + \frac{y}{4} = 1
\]

Simplify the equation:

\[
-x - \frac{y}{4} = -1
\]

Multiply both sides by \(-4\) to eliminate fractions:

\[
4x + y = 4
\]

Rearrange the terms for standard form:

\[
4x - y + 4 = 0
\]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{4x - y + 4 = 0}
\]

First, I need to determine if three points are collinear using the concept of straight lines.

I'll start by recalling that for any two points on a straight line, all other points lying on that same line will also be collinear with those two points. So, choosing two of the given points will help establish the equation of the line.

Next, I can select two points from the given three to define the line. Let's choose (3, 0) and (-2, -2). Using these two points, I'll calculate the slope of the line using the formula:

slope = (y₂ - y₁)/(x₂ - x₁)

Substituting the values:
slope = (-2 - 0)/(-2 - 3) = (-2)/(-5) = 2/5

Now that I have the slope, I can write the equation of the line in point-slope form using one of the points. Let's use (3, 0):

y - y₁ = m(x - x₁)
y - 0 = (2/5)(x - 3)

Simplifying:
y = (2/5)x - 6/5

To make calculations easier, I'll eliminate the fraction by multiplying both sides by 5:

5y = 2x - 6
Rearranging:
2x - 5y - 6 = 0

Now that I have the equation of the line passing through (3, 0) and (-2, -2), I'll check if the third point (8, 2) lies on this line. To do this, I'll substitute x = 8 into the equation:

2(8) - 5y - 6 = 0
16 - 5y - 6 = 0
10 - 5y = 0
-5y = -10
y = 2

Since substituting x = 8 gives y = 2, which matches the third point (8, 2), this confirms that all three points lie on the same straight line. Therefore, the points are collinear.
</think>

To determine if the three points \((3, 0)\), \((-2, -2)\), and \((8, 2)\) are **collinear** (lying on the same straight line), we can use the concept of slope.

### Step-by-Step Explanation:

1. **Calculate the Slope Between Two Points:**
   
   First, choose any two points to find the slope \(m\) of the line they form. Let's use \((3, 0)\) and \((-2, -2)\).
   
   \[
   m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} = \frac{-2 - 0}{-2 - 3} = \frac{-2}{-5} = \frac{2}{5}
   \]

2. **Equation of the Line:**
   
   Using the point-slope form with one of the points, say \((3, 0)\):
   
   \[
   y - y_1 = m(x - x_1) \\
   y - 0 = \frac{2}{5}(x - 3) \\
   y = \frac{2}{5}x - \frac{6}{5}
   \]
   
   To eliminate the fraction, multiply both sides by 5:
   
   \[
   5y = 2x - 6 \\
   \Rightarrow 2x - 5y - 6 = 0
   \]

3. **Check if the Third Point Lies on This Line:**
   
   Substitute \(x = 8\) into the equation to find \(y\):
   
   \[
   2(8) - 5y - 6 = 0 \\
   16 - 5y - 6 = 0 \\
   10 - 5y = 0 \\
   y = 2
   \]
   
   The third point \((8, 2)\) satisfies the equation.

### Conclusion:

Since all three points satisfy the same straight line equation \(2x - 5y - 6 = 0\), they are **collinear**.

He can sell 1080 liters at 114 rupees per liter and 1320 liters at 116 rupees per liter each week. Now, the question is asking how much he would be able to sell if the price were set at 117 rupees per liter.

Hmm, since it's a linear relationship between quantity sold (demand) and price, I think this is an application of linear demand functions in economics. The basic idea is that as the price changes, the quantity demanded changes proportionally. So, there should be a straight line relationship between these two variables: price (P) on one axis and quantity (Q) on the other.

First, let's note down the given points to form our linear demand function. At P = 114, Q = 1080 liters per week, and at P = 116, Q = 1320 liters per week. So we have two points: (114, 1080) and (116, 1320).

To find the equation of a straight line in this context, which is usually written as Q = aP + b, where 'a' is the slope and 'b' is the intercept. But since it's demand, sometimes it's also written with negative sign because higher price leads to lower quantity demanded, so maybe it should be Q = -aP + b.

Alternatively, we can think of this as P being a function of Q or vice versa. Wait, no, in linear demand functions, it's often P as a function of Q. So let me clarify: Let’s set up the equation such that for each change in price (ΔP), there is a corresponding change in quantity demanded (ΔQ).

First, calculate the slope between these two points. The formula for the slope (m) is:

m = (Change in Quantity) / (Change in Price)

So ΔQ = 1320 - 1080 = 240 liters.

To determine how many liters are sold at a price of 117 per liter, we can model the relationship between price (P) and quantity sold (S). Given two points:

- At P = 114, S = 1080
- At P = 116, S = 1320

We calculate the slope (m) of the line representing this relationship:
\[ m = \frac{1320 - 1080}{116 - 114} = \frac{240}{2} = 120 \]

Using one point to find the intercept (c):
\[ 1080 = 114(120) + c \]
\[ 1080 = 13,680 + c \]
\[ c = -12,600 \]

Thus, the equation is:
\[ S = 120P - 12,600 \]

For P = 117:
\[ S = 120(117) - 12,600 \]
\[ S = 14,040 - 12,600 \]
\[ S = 1,440 \text{ liters} \]

Answer: 1,440 liters are sold at a price of 117 per liter.

The line can have either positive intercepts of 9 each, leading to the equation \(x + y = 9\), or an intercept at (-1) on the x-axis and 1 on the y-axis, giving the equation \(x - y = -1\) (or equivalently, \(y = x + 1\)).

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Understanding the Problem:** We need to find a line that passes through the point (4,5) and has equal magnitude intercepts on both axes. This means the absolute values of the x-intercept 'a' and y-intercept 'b' are equal, so \(|a| = |b|\). However, their signs can differ.

2. **Case 1: Both Intercepts Have Same Sign (Positive):**
   - The equation is \(\frac{x}{a} + \frac{y}{a} = 1\), simplifying to \(x + y = a\).
   - Substituting the point (4,5) gives \(4 + 5 = a\), so \(a = 9\).
   - Thus, the equation is \(x + y = 9\).

3. **Case 2: Intercepts Have Opposite Signs:**
   - Let’s assume one intercept is 'a' and the other is '-a'.
   - The equation becomes \(\frac{x}{a} + \frac{y}{-a} = 1\), simplifying to \(x - y = a\).
   - Substituting (4,5) gives \(4 - 5 = a\), so \(a = -1\).
   - Therefore, the equation is \(x - y = -1\) or rearranged as \(y = x + 1\).

**Final Answer:**
The equations of the lines are \(\boxed{x + y = 9}\) and \(\boxed{y = x + 1}\).

The distance from the point (5, -7) to the line 5x - 6y - 28 = 0 can be calculated using the formula for the distance from a point to a line. 

Given:
- The line equation: \(5x - 6y - 28 = 0\)
- The point: (5, -7)

Applying the formula:
\[ \text{Distance} = \frac{|5 \times 5 + (-6) \times (-7) - 28|}{\sqrt{5^2 + (-6)^2}} \]
Calculating numerator:
\[ |25 + 42 - 28| = |39| = 39 \]

Denominator:
\[ \sqrt{25 + 36} = \sqrt{61} \]

Thus, the distance is \( \frac{39}{\sqrt{61}} \).

Final Answer: "The distance from the point (5, -7) to the line is \( \frac{39}{\sqrt{61}} \)."

To find the distance between the two parallel lines \(3x - 4y + 12 = 0\) and \(3x - 4y + 7 = 0\), we can use the formula for the distance between two parallel lines:

\[
\text{Distance} = \frac{|c_1 - c_2|}{\sqrt{a^2 + b^2}}
\]

Where:
- \( a = 3 \)
- \( b = -4 \)
- \( c_1 = 12 \)
- \( c_2 = 7 \)

\[

Alright, let's start with the given equation: 5x - 6y + 4 = 0. I think I need to rewrite this in slope-intercept form (y = mx + b) so I can easily identify the slope. So, how do I do that?

Let me subtract 5x and 4 from both sides to isolate the y term:

-6y = -5x - 4

Now, I'll divide every term by -6 to solve for y:

y = (5/6)x + (4/-6)

Wait, simplifying further, that becomes:

y = (5/6)x - (2/3)

So, the slope (m) of this line is 5/6. Since parallel lines have equal slopes, any line I find will also have a slope of 5/6.

Now, knowing that, I can use the point-slope form to write the equation of the new line passing through (-4, 5). The point-slope formula is:

y - y1 = m(x - x1)

Where (x1, y1) is the given point and m is the slope. Plugging in the values:

y - 5 = (5/6)(x - (-4))

Simplifying inside the parentheses:

y - 5 = (5/6)(x + 4)

Now, I'll distribute the 5/6 on the right side:

y - 5 = (5/6)x + (20/6)

Wait, 20 divided by 6 can be simplified. Let me reduce that fraction: both numerator and denominator are divisible by 2.

So, 20 ÷ 2 is 10, and 6 ÷ 2 is 3, giving 10/3.

Now the equation becomes:

y - 5 = (5/6)x + 10/3

Next step is to isolate y. I'll add 5 to both sides:

y = (5/6)x + 10/3 + 5

But wait, 5 can be written as a fraction over 1. To add it to 10/3, I need a common denominator. So, let me express 5 as 15/3.

So now:

y = (5/6)x + 10/3 + 15/3

Adding the fractions on the right side:

10/3 + 15/3 = 25/3

Therefore, the equation simplifies to:

y = (5/6)x + 25/3

But I think it's better to present this in standard form, which is usually Ax + By + C = 0. Let me rearrange terms accordingly.

Starting from y = (5/6)x + 25/3, subtracting both sides by y:

(5/6)x - y + 25/3 = 0

To eliminate the fractions, I'll multiply each term by 6 to make all coefficients integers. Multiplying gives:

5x - 6y + 50 = 0

Wait a second, let me check that multiplication again because when I multiplied 25/3 by 6, it should be 25*2=50, right? Because 25/3 *6 is indeed (25*6)/3 = 150/3 = 50.

So the equation in standard form is:

5x - 6y + 50 = 0

But let me verify this because I might have made a mistake during the addition earlier. Let's substitute the point (-4,5) into this equation to see if it holds true.

Plugging x=-4 and y=5:

5*(-4) -6*5 +50 = ?

Calculating each term:

5*(-4) = -20

-6*5 = -30

So, adding all terms: -20 -30 +50 = (-50) +50 = 0

Yes, that works out. So the equation is correct.

Alternatively, just to be thorough, I can express this in another form if needed, but standard form seems sufficient here.
</think>

The equation of the line parallel to \(5x - 6y + 4 = 0\) and passing through \((-4, 5)\) is:

\[
5x - 6y + 50 = 0
\]

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Find the Slope of the Given Line:**  
   Start by converting \(5x - 6y + 4 = 0\) to slope-intercept form (\(y = mx + b\)).  
   Subtract \(5x\) and add \(-4\) to both sides:  
   \[
   -6y = -5x - 4
   \]  
   Divide by \(-6\):  
   \[
   y = \frac{5}{6}x + \frac{2}{3}
   \]  
   The slope (\(m\)) is \(\frac{5}{6}\).

2. **Use Point-Slope Form:**  
   With the slope known and given point \((-4, 5)\), apply the point-slope formula:  
   \[
   y - 5 = \frac{5}{6}(x + 4)
   \]  

3. **Convert to Slope-Intercept Form:**  
   Distribute \(\frac{5}{6}\):  
   \[
   y - 5 = \frac{5}{6}x + \frac{20}{6}
   \]  
   Simplify the fraction:  
   \[
   y - 5 = \frac{5}{6}x + \frac{10}{3}
   \]  
   Add \(5\) to both sides (express \(5\) as \(\frac{15}{3}\) for easier addition):  
   \[
   y = \frac{5}{6}x + \frac{25}{3}
   \]

4. **Convert to Standard Form:**  
   Rearrange the equation and eliminate fractions by multiplying each term by \(6\):  
   \[
   5x - 6y + 50 = 0
   \]  

**Final Answer:**

The equation of the parallel line is \(\boxed{5x - 6y + 50 = 0}\).

19x + 12y + 35 = 0

Now both equations have the same coefficients for x and y, confirming they are parallel.

Next, I'll use the formula to find the distance between two parallel lines, which is |c1 - c2| divided by the square root of (a squared plus b squared). Here, a and b are the coefficients of x and y from one of the equations, and c represents the constant term after rearranging both equations into the form ax + by + c = 0.

To find the distance between two parallel lines given by the equations:

\[
19x + 12y - 38 = 0
\]
and
\[
38x + 24y + 70 = 0
\]

First, we ensure both equations are in standard form and identify their coefficients. The second equation can be simplified by dividing all terms by 2:

\[
19x + 12y + 35 = 0
\]

Now, both equations have the same coefficients for \(x\) and \(y\), confirming they are parallel.

The formula to find the distance (\(d\)) between two parallel lines \(ax + by + c_1 = 0\) and \(ax + by + c_2 = 0\) is:

\[
d = \frac{|c_1 - c_2|}{\sqrt{a^2 + b^2}}
\]

Substituting the values from our equations (\(c_1 = -38\) and \(c_2 = 35\)):

\[
d = \frac{|-38 - (-35)|}{\sqrt{19^2 + 12^2}} = \frac{|-3|}{\sqrt{361 + 144}} = \frac{3}{\sqrt{505}} \approx 0.1339
\]

**Final Answer:**

\[
\boxed{0.1339}
\]

To convert the equation \(6x + 4y - 24 = 0\) into intercept form and find its intercepts:

1. **Convert to Intercept Form**:
   Start by rearranging the equation to isolate terms with variables on one side and constants on the other.
   
   \[
   6x + 4y = 24
   \]
   
   Next, divide each term by the constant (24) to normalize the equation.

   \[
   \frac{6x}{24} + \frac{4y}{24} = \frac{24}{24}
   \]

   Simplifying gives:

   \[
   \frac{x}{4} + \frac{y}{6} = 1
   \]

   This is the intercept form of the equation, where \(a = 4\) (x-intercept) and \(b = 6\) (y-intercept).

2. **Find Intercepts**:
   - **X-Intercept**: Set \(y = 0\).
     \[
     \frac{x}{4} + \frac{0}{6} = 1 \implies x = 4
     \]
     So, the x-intercept is at \((4, 0)\).

   - **Y-Intercept**: Set \(x = 0\).
     \[
     \frac{0}{4} + \frac{y}{6} = 1 \implies y = 6
     \]
     So, the y-intercept is at \((0, 6)\).

**Final Answer:**
The equation in intercept form is \(\frac{x}{4} + \frac{y}{6} = 1\), with x-intercept at (4, 0) and y-intercept at (0, 6).

\]

To determine the distance between the two straight lines given by the equations:

\[
\text{First line: } 3x + 4y = 9
\]
\[
\text{Second line: } 6x + 8y = 18
\]

**Step 1: Simplify the Equations**

Notice that both equations can be simplified to check if they represent the same line.

- For the first equation, \(3x + 4y = 9\), it is already in standard form.
  
- For the second equation, divide all terms by 2:
  \[
  \frac{6x}{2} + \frac{8y}{2} = \frac{18}{2}
  \]
  Simplifying gives:
  \[
  3x + 4y = 9
  \]

**Step 2: Compare the Equations**

After simplification, both equations are identical:
\[
3x + 4y = 9
\]
This means both lines represent the same line. Therefore, they are coinciding lines.

**Conclusion:**

Since both equations represent the **same straight line**, there is no unique distance between them because every point on one line is also on the other line.

To find the equation of a line that is parallel to both given lines, we first note that parallel lines have the same slope.

1. **Find the slope of the given lines**:
   - For the first line (9x + 6y -7 =0):
     Convert to slope-intercept form: y = (-3/2)x + 7/6.
     Slope (m) = -3/2.
   - The second line is parallel, so it also has a slope of -3/2.

2. **Form the equation of the new line**:
   Since any line with the same slope will be parallel, we can write it in standard form as 9x +6y +k =0 (for some constant k).

3. **Choose a specific value for k to provide a unique example**:
   Let's choose k=15, resulting in 9x +6y +15=0.
   Simplifying by dividing all terms by 3: 3x +2y +5=0.

Final Answer:
The equation of the line parallel to both given lines is \boxed{3x+2y+5=0}.
